Need to promote and celebrate the Jagannath Temple in Digha as  
a major pilgrimage and Cultural Centre 
SHRIMATI SAGARIKA GHOSE (West Bengal) : Sir, Lord Jagannath Temple in Digha 
is not merely a structure of brick and mortar . It is a beacon of a civilization, a culture 
and a philosophy of faith that Bengal has nurtured for centuries. Inaugurated less than 
a year ago, built in a majestic Kalinga ar chitectural style, the temple has already 
drawn nearly one crore visitors from more than 150 countries. On Akshay Tritiya, April 
[ 11 March, 2026 ]
7

30, 2025, Bengal opened the gates of the Jagannath Temple, 213 feet of devotion 
rising from the shores of the Bay of Beng al, and the people responded.  There were 
one crore devotees in just eight months. Nearly 50,000 pilgrims visit every day, turning 
what was once a quiet seaside town into one of India's fastest rising spiritual 
destinations. Hotels are full, livelihoods are growing and the local economy is thriving.   
(MR. CHAIRMAN in the Chair.) 
This is people's faith in motion. Th is is not propaganda. This is pure bhakti. It 
is not a temple built to divide, intimidate  or score political points. Bengal said 
something very simple and very profound. We do not weaponize faith. We live it. 
Across our land, across Bengal, stand the symb ols of pluralism, the tomb of Mother 
Teresa, Nakhoda Masjid, Belur Math and ISCKON temple. Azan and temple bells, 
keertan and qawwali,  sufi, buddhist, sikh, all coexist in Bengal’s spiritual vibrancy, 
because Bengal is the land of Rama krishna Paramhansa who taught us ‘ jato mat 
thatho path’, ‘many roads, one God’.  Bengal is the land of Chaitanya Mahaprabhu 
whose message teaches us ‘humbler than gra ss, more tolerant than a tree’.  Bengal 
is the land of Swami Vivekananda who warned us that while superstition may be the 
great enemy of man, bigotry is far worse. Be ngal is the land of  Rabindranath Tagore 
who reminded us that the world is a united stream of life. For centuries our philosophy 
has echoed the ancient truth - Ekam Sat Vipra Bahudha Vadanti ; truth is one, the 
wise call it many names.  An d that truth is lived in Beng al every day. The Jagannath 
temple in Digha welcomes ev ery caste, every creed, every community; it is open and 
inclusive, without gate-keeping. Our Chief Minister's words capture perfectly 21 st 
century modern secularism – dharma jar jar, utsab shabar  – which means, 
everybody has their personal religion, bu t we celebrate festivals together. The 
Jagannath tradition is a ci vilisational bridge between Bengal and Odisha. Countless 
pilgrims undertake journey along the coast and show that there is spiritual sharing 
between Bengal and Odisha. This is Bengal 's heartbeat; people-powered devotion.

manufacture enemies.  We do not build temp les to claim monopoly over faith. The

fearless and welcoming the world, while safeguarding its plural soul. Sir, I invite every 
Member of this august House, including you,  to please visit Lord Jagannath in Digha.

Need for promoting sustainable tourism in Himachal Pradesh 
SHRI HARSH MAHAJAN (Himachal Pradesh) : Hon'ble Chairman Sir, Himachal 
Pradesh is one of India's most important to urism destinations. In last year alone, 
Himachal welcomed nearly 1.8 crore domestic and 80,000 foreign visitors. This sector 
contributes greatly to the State's GDP, sust aining lakhs of livelihood.  Himachal has 
ranked in India's most welcoming region in the last three ye ars and is among the 
global ten in the Booking.com Traveller's Review Awards. Himachal's religious 
tourism potential is immense. Sacred shrines like Jwala ji, Chintpurni, Naina Devi and 
Chamunda attracts lakhs of pilgrims alon gside the spiritual Manimahesh, Kinner 
Kailash Yatras and Chamba’s Lakshmi Narayan Temple.  Furthermore, Dharamshala, 
home to His Holiness, the Da lai Lama, and the ancient Mona steries of Lahaul Spiti, 
make the State a global hub for Buddhist to urism. The State also offers world-class 
adventure tourism for paragliding from Bir Bi lling to trekking in Spiti and Kinnaur and 
skiing in Solang Valley. I would like to thank the hon. Prime Minister and the 
Government of India for strengthening tour ism through initiatives such as Swadesh 
Darshan and PRASHAD, improving connectiv ity and promoting development.  The 
Union Budget has also announced the development of trekking routes which will 
further benefit Himachal Pradesh. However,  we must address th e weaknesses of the 
Himalayan ecosystem. Recent disasters, incl uding cloudbursts and landslides, serve 
as a stark reminder that our growth must be balanced. Challenges such as unplanned 
construction, waste management, parking and traffic congestion in our hills require 
urgent scientific intervention to protect the environment and the people. At the same 
time, tourists themselves must act responsibly. The Himalayas are not a place for 
littering plastic, damaging forests or creating disorder.  Visitors must respect local 
culture, communities and environment. I requ est the Government of India to continue 
its support by integrating environmental sa feguards and infrastructure development. 
By fostering a model of responsible and sust ainable tourism, we can ensure that the 
Dev Bhoomi remains for centuries and generations to come. Thank you.

Shri Subhasish Khuntia. 
Concern over the proposed relocation of Puri District Court 
SHRI SUBHASISH KHUNTIA (Odisha): Jai Jagannath! Thank you, Mr. Chairman, for 
giving me the opportunity to draw your ki nd attention to an urgent matter regarding 
the proposed relocation of the historic Distri ct Court of Puri to a new site near the 
Balukhand Wildlife Sanctuary by the Governme nt of Odisha. The members of the Puri 
Bar Association have been comp elled to launch a peaceful satyagraha that has now 
continued for more than 380 days, highlighting the depth of conc ern among the legal 
fraternity. The existing court complex is not merely an administrative building; it is a 
living monument of justice and history, standing for more than 150 years in the heart of 
Puri.  This historic institution has witnesse d many milestones of our promotion in the 
freedom struggle, includ ing the period of the Lavan Satyagraha .  It is also closely 
associated with Utkal Gourav Madhusud an Das, Utkal Mani Gopabandhu Das, 
Janakinath Bose, father of Netaji Subhash Chandra Bose, and President of Utkal 
Sammelani Jagabandhu Singh, who practised law here and contributed immensely to 
shaping Odisha's modern identity and stre ngthening the foundations of justice and 
rule of law in the region.  The present cour t is centrally located in the administrative 
heart of Puri, surrounded by key public inst itutions such as the Collectorate, DRDA, 
District Education Office, SP Office and many others, making it easily accessible and 
convenient for litigants and the general pub lic.  In contrast, the proposed relocation 
site reportedly falls within a sweetwater be lt, raising serious environmental and safety 
concerns. In view of the heritage signific ance of the existing building, its central 
location ensuring easy public access and the environmental sensitivity of the 
proposed site, I urge the Union Government to  intervene and ensure that this historic 
court continues to function at its presen t location since adequate space is available 
within the existing premises to sustain its operations, as is the case in several places 
[ 11 March, 2026 ]
15

in Odisha, including Bhubaneswar and Berhampur, where courts continue to function 
from their current premises. सर यहां पर हमारे ओिडशा के मंतर्ी धमȃन्दर् Ģधान जी हȅ। मȅ आपके  
माध्यम से हमारे मंतर्ी जी से भी िरक्वेÎट कर रहा हूं िक यह एक सȂिसिटव चीज है, इसके  ऊपर 
ध्यान दȂ।  जय जगन्नाथ!  बंदे उत्कल जननी!

Indira Gandhi International Airport 
DR. M. THAMBIDURAI (Tamil Nadu) : Thank you, Chairman, Sir, for giving the 
opportunity to raise an important issue re garding the traffic co ngestion on Sardar 
Patel Marg.  Sir, you know  very well, during Vajpayee ji’s period and Modi ji’s period, 
roads have been developed very well througho ut India. I was also Transport Minister. 
Present hon. Road Transport Minister, Gadkari ji, is also sitting here. Traffic 
congestion is one of the factors that affect s the area. However, Sardar Patel Marg is 
currently suitable for connecting all areas of New Delhi. But, beyond this Marg, 
National Highway starts. If we see th e Sardar Patel Marg, there are several 
encroachments, such as hotels, including st ar hotels. I do not wa nt to mention them 
specifically, but the ITC Hotel and the Taj Hotel have encroached upon several park 
[ 11 March, 2026 ]
25

areas. Because of this, while traveling to the airport, the National Highway begins 
from Patel Marg. Therefore, I request, thro ugh you, Sir, since the hon. Minister is 
present, steps be taken to take over this road. Even though, it is currently under the 
New Delhi Municipal Council area, it can be brought under the National Highway 
Authority of India and developed accordingl y. This road is extremely important for 
reaching the airport. Due to heavy traffic, many Members of Parliament also face 
difficulties, as it takes up to three hours to cross this stretch to reach the airport. 
Many other roads connect to Sardar Pa tel Marg, which further increases the 
congestion. Additionally, many international VIPs stay at ho tels in this area.  Because 
of the surrounding forest ar eas, traffic cannot be diverted through other routes.  
Meanwhile, this particular stretch has alre ady been encroached upon by others.  
Therefore, I request the hon. Minister to take over this road once again under the

Need for accelerated industrialisation in the Barak Valley in Southern Assam 
SHRI KANAD PURKAYASTHA (Assam) : Hon'ble Chairman Sir.  I rise to draw the 
urgent attention of this august House to the pressing need for accelerated 
industrialization in the Barak valley of Assam. The Barak Valley comprises of Cachar, 
Hailakandi and Sribhumi districts, surrounded by three States-Tripura, Manipur and 
Mizoram and is endowed with abundant human and natural resources. Sir, a 
populous area, with population of around 50 lakhs, has skilled and enterprising 
workforce, fertile plains fed by the Barak River, rich biodiversity, a strategic 
geographical location near international borders with Bangladesh and close proximity 
to Southeast Asia. 
Sir, with the ongoing improvement in road and rail connectivity under the 
visionary leadership of our hon. Prime Minister, Shri Narendra Modi, and the proactive 
approach of the State Government under the l eadership of hon. Chief Minister,       
Dr. Himanta Biswa Sarma ji, if the Government takes more  initiatives for accelerated 
industrialization in this area using the natural and human resource, the Barak Valley 
and the Southern Assam region can become a gateway for Northeast India's trade. 
Sir, we had Cachar paper mill under Hi ndustan Cachar Paper Corporation at 
Panchgram, which was one of the biggest in Asia. But, managerial inefficacy, political 
involvement and rampant corruption during  the Congress regime has destroyed this 
institution. The only major industry in Barak Valley is now a piece of history. 
Sir, the Barak Valley has a large segment of youth, is trained in science, 
commerce, agriculture and engineering disciplines. So, skilled and semi-skilled 
manpower, if properly harnessed, can drive manufacturing, agro-based and 
handicraft industries and services. The fertile alluvial plains of the Barak Valley support 
high value crops like pineapple, banana, black pepper, areca nut and tea estates. 
The Barak River and its tributaries provide reliable water source for irrigation and 
potential small hydro projects. The Valle y’s forests and biodiversity present 
opportunities in eco-friendly value-addition sectors like bamboo products, medicinal 
plants and allied industries.  
Sir, the Barak Valley lies close to the Silk Route Economic Belt and Southeast 
Asian markets, offering potential for expo rt-oriented unites. Hence, Sir, there is 
immense possibility for establishing manuf acturing unites, IT, logistic and green 
28
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

technologies. Although Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises (MSMEs) are present, 
but remain largely informal and unlinked to larger value chains.    
Sir, it is pertinent to mention here that the ONGC Cachar Forward Base has 
the capacity to supply more than 4 lacs SCMD (Standard Cubic Metres Per Day) of 
natural gas. But, at present, out of this capacity, only 1500 is being utilized…

ORAL ANSWERS TO QUESTIONS 
Devolution of funds in Panchayati Raj Institutions 
*196. SHRI RAGHAV CHADHA : Will the Minister of PANCHAYATI RAJ  be pleased to 
state: 
	(a)  whether Government has assessed the status of devolution of funds, 
functions and functionaries to Pancha yati Raj Institutions in the recent 
years and if so, the details thereof; 
(b)    the number of States that have  completed activity mapping and devolution 
of the funds, functions and functionaries, details thereof; 
(c)    what steps are being taken by Gove rnment to ensure effective devolution to 
Panchayats, the details thereof; and 
(d)    the outcomes of the steps taken by Government in strengthening local self-
governance, State-wise and year-wise? 
THE MINISTER OF STATE IN THE MINISTRY OF PANCHAYATI RAJ (PROF. S. P. 
SINGH BAGHEL): (a) to (d) A Statement is laid on the Table of the House. 
Statement	 
(a) to (d)   “Panchayat”, being “ Local Government”, is a State subject and 
part of State List of Seventh Schedule of th e Constitution of India. Panchayats are set 
up and operate through the respective State Panchayati Raj Acts. States have made 
their own Panchayati Raj Act, subject to the provisions of the Constitution. Article 
243G of the Constitution empowers the Legisl ature of a State to make provisions, by 
law, for the devolution of power and responsibilities upon Panchayat at appropriate 
level, subject to such conditions as may be  specified, with respect to the preparation 
of plans for economic development and social justice and implementation of schemes 
for economic development and social justice as may be entrusted to them including 
those in relation to matters listed in the Eleventh Schedule to the Constitution. The 
State legislatures are to consider the 29 su bjects listed in the Eleventh Schedule for 
devolution of power and responsibilities upon  Panchayats. Accordingly, the status of 
devolution of funds, functi ons and functionaries to Pa nchayati Raj Institutions 
depends on the extent of powers and resources devolved to them by the States 
concerned, which varies across States.  
30
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

However, the Ministry of Panchayati Ra j reviews performance of Panchayats, 
from time to time, through studies, review  meetings, field visits, video conferencing, 
Information Technology applications etc.  
The Ministry has released a report titled “Status of Devolution to Panchayats in 
States - An Indicative Evidence-Based Ranking, 2024” in February 2025 to assess the 
effectiveness of devolution and the role  of local governments  in strengthening 
grassroots democracy. This report presents the Devolution Index, which provides the 
overall scores and ranks for all States/Union  Territories covered under Part-IX of the 
Constitution, based on six identified dimensions: Framework, Functions, Finances, 
Functionaries, Capacity Enhancement, and Accountability. Ranking of States/UTs in 
Devolution Index and Sub-indices is attached as Annexure-I.  
  To strengthen the local self-governance framework of Panchayati Raj 
Institutions across the country, this  Ministry has launched eGramSwaraj 
(https://egramswaraj.gov.in), a user friendly  web-based portal, which aims to bring 
in better transparency in th e decentralized planning, progress reporting, financial 
management, work-based accounting an d details of assets created. The 
eGramSwaraj portal has also been integr ated with Public Financial Management 
System for online transfer of the Central Finance Commission funds by the States to 
PRIs and enabling Panchayats to make real-time payments to vendors/service 
providers. Panchayats prepare and uplo ad their Annual Panchayat Development 
Plans on eGramSwaraj portal. In addition , the Ministry has integrated eGramSwaraj 
with the Government e-Marketplace (GeM) to bring transparency to Panchayat 
procurement.  The status of adoption of  eGramSwaraj under XV Finance Commission 
for the year 2025-26 by States and status  of Panchayat Development Plans are at 
Annexure-II & Annexure-III respectively.  
The Ministry has implemented Mission Mode Project on e-Panchayats (MMP-
ePanchayat), a Central component of the Rashtriya Gram Swaraj Abhiyan (RGSA) 
scheme under which various e-governance projects are funded towards digitalization 
of Panchayats. Revamped Centrally Sponsored Scheme of RGSA has been 
implemented w.e.f. financial year 2022-23 in States/ Union Territories with main 
objective for capacitating PRIs through imparting training to all the Elected 
Representatives, functionaries and other stakeholders to develop their governance 
capabilities for leadersh ip roles to enable the Panchaya ts to function effectively. 
Under the scheme, Ministry supplements the efforts of States/UTs on limited scale for 
creation basic infrastructure for effective functioning of Gram Panchayats such as 
[ 11 March, 2026 ]
31

Construction of Gram Panchayat Bhawans, Computers and Colocation of Common 
Service Centres (CSC) with Gram Pancha yat Bhawans with focus on North East 
States, as proposed by States/UTs in their Annual Action Plans and later approved by 
Central Empowered Committee. Ministry has also been providing support to the 
States/UTs, under the scheme of reva mped RGSA for setting up of Project 
Management Units (PMUs) at States, Distri ct and Block level as approved in the 
Annual Action Plan for effective implementa tion of the scheme and setting up of 
institutional mechanism as State, Dist rict and Block level Panchayat Resource 
Centres to support Capacity Building & Training of Panchayats.  
An application of ‘AuditOnline’ has been developed for online audits of 
Panchayat accounts and their financial ma nagement. AuditOnline for transparent 
auditing of Central Finance Commission f unds utilization for strengthen financial 
management of Panchayats launched in April 2020. For au dit year 2023-24, 2.58 lakh 
Audit Plans have been created, and 2.56 la kh Audit Report have been generated. 
State wise details may be seen at Annexure-IV. Furthermore, for the audit year 2024-
25, 2.12 lakh Audit Plans have been created, and 1.54 lakh Audit Reports have been 
generated. The State wise details may be seen at Annexure- V. 
Similarly, Panchayat NIRNAY is an onlin e application that aims at bringing 
transparency and better management in co nduct of Gram Sabhas by Panchayats. 
The Ministry has also launched SabhaSaar,  an AI-enabled meeting summarisation 
tool, on 14th August 2025. The SabhaSaar platform enables structured capture and 
analysis of Gram Sabha proceedings and supports monitoring of key parameters such 
as frequency, type of Gram Sabha meetings and participation.

from Delhi, and going towards South. Tr affic goes to Surat, Vadodara, Ahmedabad 
and, after that, it goes to Mumbai. From Mu mbai, it goes to Pune, and, then, from 
Pune, it goes to Kolhapur and also to Solapu r. But, as the first important thing, we 
have taken a historic decision to make an express highway from Mumbai to Delhi. It is 
already 70 to 80 per cent complete. After Surat, we are making new green express 
highway. Starting from Surat, it goes to Nashik, Nashik to Ahmednagar, Ahmednagar 
to Solapur. It then goes to Akkalkot and Kurnool. From Kurnool, it connects Chennai, 
Kanyakumari, Bangalore, Mangalore, Hyderabad and Cochin. So, it is a new 
connectivity from North India to South India. So, it is better and it is going to reduce 
the distance between Delhi and Chennai by 320 kilometers, which is going to reduce 
the cost also. Because of the highway, it will reduce the time period also.

Underfunding of Garima Grehs for transgender persons 
198. DR. FAUZIA KHAN: Will the Minister of SOCIAL JUSTICE AND 
EMPOWERMENT be pleased to state: 
(a)    whether it is a fact that Garima Greh shelters under the SMILE initiative are 
facing delays and shortfalls in Go vernment funding, leading to staff 
attrition, closure threats and curtailed capacity; 
(b)   if so, the reasons for erratic disb ursal of funds and bureaucratic hurdles 
cited by shelter operators and measur es being taken to streamline funding 
processes and prevent operational shutdowns; 
(c)   whether the Ministry has establis hed or plan to es tablish a dedicated 
grievance redressal system and regu larized timelines for staff salary 
payments in Garima Grehs; and 
(d)      if so, the details thereof and if not, the reasons therefor? 
THE MINISTER OF STATE IN THE MINISTRY OF SOCIAL JUSTICE AND 
EMPOWERMENT (SHRI B. L. VERMA): (a) to (d) A statement is laid on the Table of 
the House. 
Statement 
(a) and (b) No, Madam. Support for Ma rginalised Individuals for Livelihood 
and Enterprise (SMILE) is a comprehensive Scheme for rehabilitat ion for welfare of 
transgender persons, which was launched on 12.02.2022.  
58
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

One of the components of the Scheme, "Housing in the form of ‘Garima Greh’ 
for Transgender Persons", is to prov ide support to the Community Based 
Organisations (CBOs)/ Non-Government Organisations (NGOs), for running Garima 
Grehs, including salary for the staff, rent for building and administrative expenses. In 
addition to this, non-recurring funds are also released for setting up Garima Grehs. 
Funds are released to Garima Greh after processing of their proposals as per 
the Scheme guidelines and on submission of mandatory documents. Funds to these 
Garima Grehs are released through E-Anudaan in two instalments of 50% each. 
 Further, for streamlining funding proce sses and to resolve issues faced by 
Garima Grehs, review meetings by National Council for Transgender Persons 
(NCTP), National Conferences, Regional Re view Meetings and Zonal Meetings are 
conducted on regular intervals. Moreover , stakeholder's consultation meet, Video 
Conferences and handholding training sessions are organised by the Department with 
the Garima Greh functionaries. 
(c) and (d) No, Madam. However, grievances relating to Garima Grehs, 
including those raised by staff, are ad dressed through the Centralised Public 
Grievance Redress and Moni toring System (CPGRAMS). CPGRAMS provides an 
integrated digital platform for registration , monitoring and time-bound disposal of 
grievances, with defined timelines, an es calation mechanism and system-generated 
alerts for effective redressal. 
In addition, grievances may also be  received through other channels, 
representations from concerned staff thro ugh letters or emails, and communications 
from social workers or other stakeholders. 
Further, the Department conducts peri odic inspections of Garima Grehs. 
During such inspections, the inspecting offi cers also interact with the staff members 
to ascertain any issues faced by them, including those related to service conditions. 
DR. FAUZIA KHAN : Sir, the September 2025 Report  of the NHRC on Garima Grehs 
documents an average delay of 15 months in fund disbursement to shelters which has 
led to self-financing by NGOs, eviction threats and inadequate living conditions for the 
residents. Will the Minister explain the reas ons for such chronic delays despite the 
Ministry’s reimbursement-based model, while the answer written to me is, ‘No’?  Sir, 
in light of the expansion of Garima Grehs to 23 operational shelters in 2026, may I 
know from the hon. Minister whether any instances of delayed staff payments or 
[ 11 March, 2026 ]

disruption in operational funding have come  to the notice of the Ministry? And if so,

particularly, what specific accountability measures such as mandatory quarterly audits 
or penalties for delayed releases has the Ministry introduced since the NHRC advisory 
of September 2023? And how many complaints of funding-related attrition or reduced 
resident intake have been received in the last two years?

SHRI P. P. SUNEER (Kerala) : Thank you, Sir, for giving me the opportunity to 
participate in the discussion on the Workin g of the Ministry of Rural Development. At 
the outset, I would like to emphasize that rural development is not merely a budgetary 
exercise. It is the backbone of our nation's  inclusive growth. More than two-thirds of 
our population lives in rural areas, and their livelihoods, infrastructure, and social 
security depend significantly on the policie s and allocations made by this Ministry. 
Rural India continues to face multiple ch allenges, unemployment, agrarian distress, 
lack of infrastructure and widening inequalities. As per the Union Budget 2026-27, the 
Budget Estimate for the Ministry of Rural Development in 2025-2026 was Rs. 
2,86,754.53 crore, but in 2026-27, the Budget Estimate is just Rs. 2,40,768.81 crore, 
which clearly indicates a huge decline in the allocation for the Ministry of Rural 
Departments. One of the most importan t programmes under the Ministry has been 
the Mahatma Gandhi National Rural Empl oyment Guarantee Scheme. This scheme 
has been a lifeline for millio ns of rural households. Ho wever, we repeatedly see 
situations where funds are exhausted before the end of the financial year, resulting in 
delayed wage payments and reduced workdays for labourers. Now, this scheme itself 
has been replaced by the newly introduced Viksit Bharat Guarantee for Rozgar and 
Aajeevika Mission (Gramin), VB-G RAM G, with only 60 per cent assistance from the 
Centre. Sir, employment guarantee is not charity; it is a legal right of the rural 
workers. Whatever be the scheme, delaye d wages defeat the very spirit of the 
scheme. I urge the Government to ensure adequate and timely funding for the above 
scheme, so that workers receive their wages without delay and guaranteed number of 
workdays is actually provided. 
Sir, another concern is the delay in release of  funds to States and 
implementing agencies. Development pr ogrammes succeed only when funds reach 
the grassroots in time. Greater transparen cy, simplified procedures and cooperative 
federalism are essential to ensure that States and local bodies implement these 
schemes effectively. 
Finally, Sir, I urge the Government to ensure adequate funding, timely wage 
payments under employment schemes, improv ed support for rural housing, stronger 
infrastructure development and greater em ployment of self-help groups and local 
institutions. If rural India prospers, Indi a prospers. Strengthen ing our villages means 
strengthening the foundation of our nation. Thank you, Sir. 
THE VICE-CHAIRMAN (DR. DINESH SHARMA):  Thank you, Shri P. P. Suneer. The 
next speaker is Shrimati Rekha Sharma – ten minutes.

Madam, as a large share of India's popu lation continues to reside in rural 
areas, the Union Budget allocation for Ru ral Development increased from Rs.87,765 
crore in 2016-17 to Rs.2,73, 108 crore in 2026-27, representi ng a rise of over 211 per 
cent during the decade. These figures indi cate a sustained fiscal commitment to 
strengthening rural infrastructure, livelihoods and local institutional capacity. Major 
national programmes such as the Viksit Bharat-Guarantee for Rozgar and Ajeevika 
Mission (Grameen), formerly MGNREGA, Deen Dayal Antyodaya Yojana-National 
Rural Livelihoods Mission, Swachh Bharat Mission and Jal Jeevan Mission have 
embedded participatory mechanism by wo rking through Panchayats, Self-Help 
Groups and grassroots organizations. Direct fiscal transfers to Panchayats have been 
increased from around Rs. 2.36 lakh crore under the 15th Finance Commission (2021-
2026) to nearly Rs. 4.3 lakh crore under the 16 th Finance Commission (2026-2031), 
thereby significantly enhancing local financial autonomy.  
Madam, under the leadership of Shri  Narendra Modi, India has recorded 
substantial advances in expanding social protection and reducing poverty. Coverage 
under social protection systems, which incl udes access to improved drinking water, 
household electrification and rural sanitati on, increased from 22 per cent in 2016 to 
64.3 per cent in 2025, reflecting a significant broadening of social security reach.  
Madam, India's Multidimensional Poverty Index published by NITI Aayog 
indicates Poverty Headcount Ratio declined  from 55.3 per cent in 2005-06 to 14.96 
per cent in 2019-2021, and further to 11.28 per cent in 2022-23. These trends suggest 
that sustained economic growth, in conjunction with targeted social welfare 
interventions and expanded access to basic services, has contributed to a significant 
reduction in poverty in India. Under the lead ership of Shri Narendra Modi, the public 
policy in India is oriented towards expanding equal opportunities, reducing absolute 
poverty and mitigating the risk of widening inequality. The Government initiatives have 
focused on expanding access to affordable  housing, food and social security, 
financial inclusion, universal basic services and affordable healthcare. Together, these 
interventions have played a central role in improving living standards, particularly in 
rural areas. Jal Jeevan Mission wa s launched in 2019 to achieve Har Ghar Jal . At 
inception, only 3.23 crore rural househol ds, about 17 per cent, had tap water 
connections. By November, 2025, an addi tional 12.50 crore households were 
covered, raising total coverage to about 15.74 crore households.   
 The Swachh Bharat Mission was launched in 2014 to achieve Open Defecation 
Free (ODF) status nationwide by October,  2019. As of Decembe r 31, 2025, over 96 
128
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

per cent of villages under the Swachh Bharat Mission have attained ODF Plus status. 
An ODF Plus village is defined as one that  sustains its ODF status, ensures solid and 
liquid waste management and remains visua lly clean. As of February 9, 2026, there 
are 5.68 lakhs ODF Plus villages in our co untry, of which 5.29 lakhs have suitable 
arrangement for solid waste management an d 5.46 lakhs have suitable arrangement 
for liquid waste management.   
 Pradhan Mantri Sahaj Bijli Har Ghar Yojana (SAUBHAGYA) was launched in 
2017 to achieve universal household electrifi cation by providing electricity connections 
to all willing un-electrified households in ru ral areas and all willing poor households in 
urban areas in the country. In 2017, the total number of un-electrified households was 
estimated at three crores, against which 2.86 crores households across the country 
were electrified by Financial Year 2021-22.  As of March 31, 2019, all States have 
reported 100 per cent electrification of all the willing un-electrified households 
identified. 
 Implemented since April, 2016, the Pradhan Mantri Awas Yojana-Gramin  
(PMAY-G) aims to achieve the objective of “Housing for All ” by 2029 by providing 
pucca dwellings with basic amenities to eligible rural households. Against an overall 
target of 4.95 crores houses, 4.14 crores units have been allocated to States and 
Union Territories, of which 2.93 crores have been completed to date. The total 
number of rural houses constructed over th e past 11 years is 3.70 crores. Reflecting 
the heightened policy emphasis on rural housing, budgetary allocations for PMAY-G 
increased from Rs.15,000 crore in 2016-17 to Rs.54,916.70 crore in 2026-27. 
 Madam, rural connectivity is a foundationa l pillar of rural development, directly 
contributing to poverty reduction by e nhancing access to healthcare, education, 
markets and employment opportunities. Th e Pradhan Mantri Gram Sadak Yojana 
(PMGSY-I) was designed to provide all- weather road connectivity to eligible 
unconnected habitations. As of mid-January 2026, more than 99.6 per cent of eligible 
habitations had been connected. Under Pradhan Mantri Gram Sadak Yojana-I, a total 
of 1,63,665 roads spanning 6.25 lakh kilometr es, along with 7,210 bridges, have been 
completed.   
 The Pradhan Mantri Gram Sadak Yojana (PMGSY-II) focused on 
strengthening and upgrading the existing ru ral road network.  As of mid-January 
2026, a total of 6,612 roads covering 49,087  kilometres, along with 749 bridges, had 
been completed under this phase. The Pr adhan Mantri Gram Sadak Yojana – IV 
[ 11 March, 2026 ]
129

approved for the years 2024-29, aims to construct 62,500 kilometres to connect 
25,000 new habitations. So far, 12,100 kilometres of roads have been approved.   
 Madam, reflecting sustained policy emph asis on rural connectivity, budgetary 
allocations for Pradhan Mantri Gram Sadak Yojana increased from Rs.12,581 crore in 
2016-17 to Rs.19,000 crore in 2026-27. Targ eted infrastructure interventions have 
increasingly prioritized tribal and particularly vulnerable communities.

Need for a mandatory national legal framework to address bullying and  
cyberbullying in schools 
SHRIMATI SUDHA MURTHY (Nominated):  I draw the attentio n of the Government 
towards the serious issue of bullying an d cyber-bullying among school children. 
Recent tragedies across the country have shown that this is not just a personal matter 
between children, but a failure of instit utional accountability. The PARAKH Rashtriya 
Sarvekshan, 2024 report by NCERT highlights the scale of this crisis. It reveals that 32 
per cent of the students in Indian school s are teased or mocked, 24 per cent face 
physical pushing or hitting, and 22 per cent are threatened by their peers. Alarmingly, 
only 45 per cent of the students find it comf ortable to talk to someone when they feel 
upset. Despite these figures, only 70 per ce nt of schools have reported having an 
anti-bullying policy in place. 
While the National Education Policy, 2020 emphasizes mental health, many 
schools still lack the necessary counsellors. The Manodarpan tele-helpline has 
already extended guidance to over 3.2 lakh calls, which clearly indicates the immense 
psychological needs of our students. Howe ver, a helpline is a support tool, not a 
substitute for on-ground safety. Currently, there are only advisory guidelines from the 
National Commission for Protection of Child Rights and the Ministry of Education. As 
there is no mandatory national law, implem entation is inconsistent across different 
States and school boards. 
I humbly request the Government to en act a mandatory national framework 
that includes emotional sensitivity, training in the curriculum and clear and time-
bound reporting mechanism. Every school must be legally obligated to provide a safe 
and dignified environment to fulfill our commitment to the welfare of our children. 
Thank you, Madam, Jai Hind.

supply quality generic medicine at an affordab le cost. To take this even closer to the 
people, there is an urgent need to proliferate Jan Aushadi Kendras across urban, rural 
and remote corners of all the States. In this effort to build physical infrastructure and 
network for Jan Aushadi Kendras, I feel th at collaboration is necessary between the 
Health Ministry and the Department of Post s which has more than 25,000 post offices 
across the nation. The generic medicine shop s can be set up in post office buildings 
and vacant lands belonging to the postal de partment. This collaboration will further 
help to rebrand the postal department. Also , the postal department can be utilized to 
transport medicines from warehouses to Jan Aushadhi Kendras across the nation.

be used for doorstep delivery of Jan Au shadhi medicines too. Patients can place

Concern over India's missing protocol on Maternal Mental Health 
DR. FAUZIA KHAN (Maharashtra):  Thank you, Madam, for giving me the 
opportunity.  Today, I rise to address a silent crisis that touches every district of this 
nation, yet remains largely invisible within  our health infrastr ucture. Recent meta-
analyses estimate postpartum depression affects between 19 and 23.5 per cent of 
Indian mothers, figures climb to nearly 30 per cent in certain rural and peri-urban 
settings. Far too many women carry this hidden burden in silence, across countless 
cradles, with no trained hand extended towards them. The consequences are grave. 
Impaired mother-infant bonding, comp romised child cognitive development, 
136
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

deepening family strain, the quiet anguish behind closed  doors in our villages and 
cities alike represent a public health con cern we can no longer defer. India has no 
standardised national protocol for routine perinatal mental health screening. Mothers 
deserve more than survival. Our existing infrastructure, Ayushman Bharat's Health 
and Wellness Centres, Pradhan Mantri Surakshit Matritva Abhiyan , ASHA and ANM 
networks offer a ready platform. The Edinburgh Postnatal Depression Scale, adapted 
and validated for Indian contexts, can be integrated affordably.  
I urge the Government and the Minister present here  to develop a national 
perinatal mental health protocol, mandate ro utine screening within existing maternal 
schemes, and equip frontline workers with training and referral pathways in full 
alignment with WHO recommendations. Thank you, Madam.

Concern over repeated disturbances of Indo-Myanmar Border fencing along  
Manipur Sector 
SHRI MAHARAJA SANAJAOBA LEISHEMBA (Manipur) : Thank you, Madam, for 
giving me the opportunity.  India shares  a 1,643 Km long border with Myanmar 
passing four North Eastern  States – Ar unachal Pradesh, Nagaland, Manipur and 
Mizoram -- and Manipur alone shares ar ound 398 Km with Myanmar. Previously, 
there was no border fencing and Free Move ment Regime was implemented along this 
border. But, due to the rise in insurgent activities, cross-border infiltration, drug 
smuggling and other illegal activities, the Go vernment of India started border fencing 
on war footing and,  as of September, 2024, more than 30 Km long border fencing 
work has already been completed and the Free Movement Regime has also been 
scrapped. However, the highest number of disturbances related to border fencing are 
being reported in Manipur sector. In Decem ber 2025, iron fencing poles at the border 
of Chandel District, Manipur, were cut twic e. Again, similar incidents were reported 
along the border of Tengnoupal District , Manipur in August, 2025, and equipment 
used for fencing construction were also found stolen. Again, in January, 2026, 47 iron 
poles of approximately 150 metres of fenc ing were found damaged at the border of 
Chandel District. Furthermore, up to the end of February, 2026, 11 IEDs were 
[ 11 March, 2026 ]
137

detected and diffused along the Indo-Myanmar Border in Tengnoupal District, 
Manipur. These incidents are willful disturbances posing serious challenges to the 
National Security. I urge the Government to thoroughly examine the matter and take 
appropriate and timely action, so that th e border fencing work could be continued 
without any hindrance and completed at the earliest. Thank you, Madam.

138
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

is, the matter of airport auth ority clearances for building construction. At present, the

submitted for No Objection Certificate (NOC) from Airports Authority of India has led

Demand for inclusion of tenant farmers from Goa under PM-KISAN  
Samman Nidhi Scheme 
SHRI SADANAND MHALU SHET TANAVADE (Goa):  Madam, I draw attention of the 
Government to the urgent need for inclus ion of tenant farmers from Goa under the 
PM-KISAN Samman Nidhi Scheme. This flagship initiative of the Government of India 
has been transformative for farmers acro ss the country, providing crucial income 
support, and has been widely appreciated. Goa has approximately 52,000 cultivating 
farmers. However, due to the existing elig ibility condition requiring land ownership, 
only about 6,000 farmers in the State curr ently benefit from the scheme. Under the 
Goa Agricultural Tenancy Act, 1964, tenant  cultivators enjoy rights equivalent to 
landowners for cultivation. They are treat ed as deemed owners  under State law and 
are eligible for several Stat e agricultural schemes. Currently, over 20,000 farmers in 
Goa are registered as tenant farmers, inheri tors of tenants, or in similar categories. 
Inclusion of these tenant farmers under PM-KISAN would significantly broaden the 
scheme’s reach, benefiting a large number of  small and marginal farmers, who form 
more than 80 percent of Goa’s agricu ltural community. For them, the annual 
assistance of Rs. 6,000 is vital to cover in put costs and cope with distress situations. 
Agriculture in Goa is not only critical for food security but also preserves the State’s 
natural landscape, which supports its tourism-driven economy. I request the 
Government to kindly approve the inclusio n of tenant farmers from Goa under the 
PM-KISAN scheme, so that all deserving cultivators get the support they need. 
THE VICE-CHAIRPERSON (SHRIMATI PHANGNON S. KONYAK):  Hon. Member, 
Dr. John Brittas (Kerala), associated himsel f with the Special Mention made by hon. 
Member, Shri Sadanand Mhalu Shet Tanavade.  
Now, Dr. John Brittas.

Demand for setting up AIIMS in Kozhikode, Kerala 
DR. JOHN BRITTAS (Kerala): Madam, Vice-Chairperson, in an effort to address 
regional disparities in the accessibility and affordability of advanced tertiary healthcare 
services across the nation, Government has been establishing new AIIMSs in various 
States under Pradhan Mantri Swasthya Sura ksha Yojana (PMSSY) Initiative. A total 
of 22 AIIMS have been announ ced so far under PMSSY in various States. Kerala is, 
perhaps, the only major State that does not have an AIIMS. The demand to set up an 
AIIMS in Kerala dates back over a decade. Despite Kerala's remarkable achievements 
in healthcare, it lacks a Union Government-f unded institution of national Importance 
for tertiary healthcare and res earch. Kerala identified Kinalu r in Kozhikode district as 
the proposed site for AIIMS, which is having all required facilities to cater to the 
requirements for establishment of AIIMS, and formally communicated the same to 
Centre requesting sanction. In answers to written Questions raised by me in Rajya 
Sabha on 25.07.2023 and 03.12.2024, Government revealed that the proposal for 
AIIMS in Kerala has not been approved under the current phase of PMSSY. However, 
the Minister of Health and Family Welfare had assured in Rajya Sabha, during its 265 th 
Session, that Kerala is being considered for setting up an AIIMS. Therefore, I 
earnestly urge upon the Government to  expeditiously grant approval for the 
establishment of AIIMS at Kinalur in Kozhikode district in Kerala.  
THE VICE-CHAIRPERSON (SHRIMATI S. PHANGNON KONYAK) : The following 
hon. Members associated themselves with  the Special Mention made by the hon. 
Member, Dr. John Brittas: Shrimati Rajathi (Tamil Nadu), Shri Haris Beeran (Kerala) 
and Shri P. P. Suneer (Kerala). Shri Sanjay Raut; not present.  
Now, Dr. Medha Vishram Kulkarni. 
Demand for inclusion of Alandi under the Temple Town  
Development Framework 
DR. MEDHA VISHRAM KULKARNI (Maharashtra) : Madam, Vice-Chairperson, I 
demand for the inclusion of Alandi, the sacred temple town of Marathi Sant 
Dnyaneshwar Maharaj in Pune district , Maharashtra, under the Temple Town 
Development framework announced by the Un ion Government. Alandi is one of the 
most revered pilgrimage centres in the country and holds exceptional spiritual, 
cultural and historical importance. It wi tnesses the arrival of lakhs of devotees 
annually, particularly during the Ashadhi and Kartiki Wari, placing immense pressure 
on civic infrastructure, sanitation, tr affic management, public amenities and 
142
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

environmental resources. Despite its national  spiritual significance, Alandi continues 
to face serious challenges arising from  unplanned urbanisation and inadequate 
infrastructure. The recent Union Budget’s emphasis on strengthening infrastructure in 
temple towns and Tier-II cities provides a timely and appropriate policy opportunity to 
address these long-standing issues. Inclusio n of Alandi under this framework will 
enable focused investments in pilgrim fac ilities, sanitation, sewage and solid waste 
management, riverfront development, flood co ntrol, public transport, parking, road 
connectivity and heritage conservation. Further, Alandi’s identity is inseparably linked 
with the Indrayani River, which requires ur gent rejuvenation and pollution control. 
Temple town status will facilitate integrated  planning for river restoration, alongside 
urban development, ensuring environmental sustainability and public health. 
I, therefore, urge the Government to formally include Alandi under the Temple 
Town Development Programme, so that its spiritual legacy is preserved while 
ensuring modern infrastructure, improved qu ality of life for residents and a dignified 
experience for pilgrims from across the country.             
THE VICE-CHAIRPERSON (SHRIMATI S. PHANGNON KONYAK) : The following 
hon. Members associated themselves with  the Special Mention made by the hon. 
Member, Dr. Medha Vishram Kulkarni: Shri mati Maya Naroliya (Madhya Pradesh) 
and Shri Sat Paul Sharma (Jammu and Kashmir).
